                                        SCOPE AND OBJECTIVES



                                                    Scope




(1)                 The Auditor General, as part of the Legislature’s oversight responsibility for

       operations of State agencies, is responsible for reviews of electronic data processing systems.

       Our review of the Florida On-Line Recipient Integrated Data Access (FLORIDA) System of the

       Florida Department of Health and Rehabilitative Services, hereafter referred to as "the

       Department," was conducted in accordance with GOVERNMENT AUDITING STANDARDS

       issued by the Comptroller General of the United States.       The proper administration of public

       funds requires that management establish and maintain an internal control structure, including

       the control environment, accounting system, and policies and procedures, to provide reasonable

       assurance that specific entity objectives will be achieved.



 (2)                Our review included an evaluation of selected control environment factors and

       policies and procedures of the Department’s internal control structure relative to the FLORIDA

       System during the period April 1, 1992, through October 14, 1992, and selected Department

       actions taken through February 12, 1993. This included a review of:



         @   The Department’s methods of acquiring computer software and systems development

             services for the FLORIDA System.



         @ The provisions and administration ofthe Department’s contract with Electronic Data Systems

             Federal Corporation (EDS) for the development of the FLORIDA System.



         @ The Department’s long-term plan for the implementation of the FLORIDA System and the
             progress towards its accomplishment.

                                                                                    g to access,

       Selected computer application and user control procedures, including certain input,

       processing, and output controls within the FLORIDA System’s components.




                                             Objectives



(3)            Our review objectives were:



       To determine if the Department has complied with laws, regulations, and
                                                                               administrative

       rules relating to the acquisition of computer software and systems development
                                                                                        services for
       the FLORIDA System.




       To determine the adequacy of the Department’s procedures, records, and document

                                                                                               ent of
       the FLORIDA System.




       To determine if the internal control structure associated with the FLORIDA
                                                                                  System,

       including selected general, application, and user control procedures, is adequatel
                                                                                            y designed
       and operating effectively.



       To determine if the FLORIDA System as currently designed, including the selection

       computer hardware and software, will sufficiently meet the Department's requiremen
                                                                                                ts and

       objectives to the extent that the System’s continued development and implementation should

       proceed under the current development strategy.

## Page 11

                                            METHODOLOGY



(4)                As part of our review, we interviewed appropriate personnel of the Department’s

      Office of Management Systems, Economic Services and Child Support Enforcement Program

      Offices, and Office of Internal Audit; surveyed selected users of the FLORIDA System; reviewed
      the Department’s written policies, procedures, and applicable system and other documentation;
      and made observations to determine whether the prescribed control procedures had been placed
      in operation. In addition, we tested selected control policies and procedures to determine whether
      they were adequately designed and operating effectively.

                                              BACKGROUND




                                                   General



(5)                   In their reviews and analyses of internal operations conducted in late 1984 and

       subsequently, Department staff determined that the Department's operating environment was

       characterized by two significant deficiencies.   These included:



         ©   Inefficiencies caused by multiple data processing systems in the various public assistance

             and child support enforcement programs, including cumbersome or absent interfaces among

             programs and systems and




         e A   lack of automated features in existing data processing systems which resulted in the

             performance of labor intensive and error-prone activities by Department personnel.




(6)                 Consequently, during 1987 and 1988, Department management, with indications of

       appropriate Federal and State funding, made the decision to procure and implement a new on-line

       integrated system that would eliminate the inefficiencies noted above and facilitate the provision

       of services by the Department.    The system purchased, termed the Florida On-Line Recipient

       Integrated Data Access (FLORIDA) System, is a Statewide system that was designed to combine

       information and processing functions for the Department’s Aid to Families with Dependent

       Children (AFDC), Medicaid, Food Stamps, Refugee Assistance, and Child Support Enforcement

       (CSE) programs, into one system.     The FLORIDA System also was designed to perform the

       functions formerly performed by the Department’s Income and Eligibility Verification System

       (IEVS) and Overpayment, Fraud, and Recoupment Case Management System.             The FLORIDA

       System is functionally organized into two distinct parts: Public Assistance (PA) which consists

       of the AFDC, Food Stamps, Medicaid, and Refugee Assistance programs and CSE.

---

## Page 13

                      Department management made the determination to acquire a Statewide on-line

       integrated system through competitive solicitation, using the Request for Proposal (RFP) process.

       To assist the Department in acquiring the necessary system and services, the Department procured

       the services of a consultant to aid in the preparation of the RFP and applicable planning

       documents.      On April   14,   1988, the RFP received approval from the State Information

       Technology Resource Procurement Advisory Council (ITRPAC). The RFP received necessary

       approvals from the United States Department of Agriculture’s (USDA) Food and Nutrition Service

       (FNS) and United States Department of Health and Human Services (DHHS) on April 13, 1988,

       and May 9, 1988, respectively.




 (8)                  The RFP was officially advertised and issued to prospective vendors on May 20,

       1988.    The Department received proposals from the following companies:



         @     CSX Technology; Jacksonville, Florida.



         @     Electronic Data Systems Federal Corporation (EDS); Herndon, Virginia.



         @     Unisys Corporation; Blue Bell, Pennsylvania.



(9)                   Through the evaluation process, the Department selected EDS as the prime contractor

       on November 28, 1988. However, in December 1988, Unisys Corporation filed a protest relating

       to the Department’s proposal evaluation process and EDS’ proposal. This protest was resolved

       in March 1989 through the State’s Administrative Hearing process. The contract with EDS was

       then approved by the Governor and Cabinet on April 11, 1989, and signed by both parties in May

       1989.     The contract was a fixed price, single vendor contract that provided for system

       development, hardware, a telecommunications network, training and conversion activities, system

       implementation, and facilities management.       EDS, as prime contractor, subcontracted work to

       International Business Machines (IBM) Corporation, Deloitte and Touche (formerly Touche Ross),

       IV-D Systems, Inc., MIS Software Development, Inc. (MSD), and Florida State University.

                                               System Description



(10)                 The FLORIDA System was designed to facilitate and economize the provision of

       services through the creation of a single, centralized data processing system.               The System
       maintains applicant/client and program information that may be electronically accessed and
                                                                                                              used
       by various programs and offices. The FLORIDA System was designed to allow an applicant for

       benefits to visit one of the Department’s service centers, complete one application package, and

       be determined eligible or ineligible for all available programs (i.e., AFDC, Food Stamps,

       Medicaid, CSE, etc.) at the same time.        For those programs that provide for cash assistance to

       eligible individuals, the FLORIDA System generates a computer tape that is submitted to the State

       Comptroller for the generation of applicable benefit warrants.




(11)                 The    Department   initially   projected   in    March   1989   that   the   purchase   and

       implementation of the FLORIDA System would cost approximately $81.1                     million.   As of
       December 31, 1992, the Department's records disclosed that approximately $104.4 million
                                                                                               had

       been expended, with the Federal Government contributing approximately 75 percent to that cost

       and the State paying the remaining 25 percent.        In March 1993 the Department projected that

       the cost of development and implementation through March 31, 1993, would be approximately

       $109 million.      Because the conditions and requirements necessary to obtain final Federal

       certification are currently unknown, Department staff indicated that a projection/determinatio
                                                                                                                n

       of final costs cannot currently be calculated.



(12)                 The FLORIDA System’s hardware configuration consists of International Business

       Machines (IBM) ES3090/600J and ES9021/900 central processing units (CPU).                    The System

       includes data files that store recipient/client information that has been entered either on-line or

       from data tapes.    It uses the information on the data files to determine, among other things, the

       recipients’/clients’ eligibility for benefits.    As of January 21,       1993,   the Department had

       approximately 6,678 terminals and        1,480 personal        computers (PC) distributed throughout

       Department Headquarters and the || districts for accessing the FLORIDA System. Security over

       access to FLORIDA System data files and programs is handled through a security software

---

## Page 15

Par.
No.


        package purchased by the Department. The name of this security software is Resource Access
        Control Facility (RACF).



                                            System Implementation



(13)                 The Department initially planned to implement (i.e., convert from the Department’s
        former data processing systems to the FLORIDA System) the FLORIDA System during the period
        May 1, 1991, through September 16, 1991. In addition, the DHHS initially planned to perform
        a postinstallation certification review by February 1, 1992.       To receive maximum Federal
        participation in funding the System’s implementation costs, the Department must receive a
        satisfactory rating after the completion of the certification review. The implementation schedule
        and the related Federal postinstallation certification review have been delayed. These delays have
        been attributed by the Department to program changes mandated by the Federal Family Support
        Act, significant changes (i.e., increases) in the originally planned procurement activities, and the
        Department having to assume all operations of the System from the contractor (i.e., EDS) earlier
        than anticipated.



 (14)                As of November 20, 1992, all of the Department’s districts were using the
        FLORIDA System; however, not all districts had converted their entire case load to the System.
        According to the Department’s records, the percentage of completion for the case load conversion
        as of January 1, 1993, ranged from 88.5 percent in District XI to 100 percent in District I. In
        addition, the CSE Financial Management module was not complete as of that date.
        Correspondence submitted by the Department to the applicable Federal agencies in July and
        August 1992 indicated that the Department plans to complete the implementation of the Public
        Assistance module and be prepared for DHHS’ postinstallation certification review by March 30,
        1993; and plans to complete the CSE financial management module by August 1993.

         e@   "for penalties wrongfully withheld by HRS from payments due EDS under the FLORIDA

(16)                 On September 14, 1992, the Department filed a Motion to Dismiss the Complaint,

       Motion to Strike claims of interest and certain allegations in the Complaint that the Department

       considers impertinent and immaterial, and Motion for More Definite Statement with the Circuit

       Court of the Second Judicial Circuit, in and for Leon County, Florida. The Motion to Dismiss

       was based on the Department’s claim that EDS did not follow the dispute resolution procedures

       referred to in the written contract; that EDS did not exhaust available administrative remedies;

       and that EDS’ service of process regarding the complaint was defective.



(17)                 As of January 11, 1993, this case was still pending before the court.



                                               Other Matters




(18)                 General operations (i.e., processing of live data) for the FLORIDA System are

       currently conducted using the IBM ES9021/900 CPU. This CPU was installed by IBM in August

       1992 as an upgrade to the previous CPU (i.e., IBM 9000/720), which had been deemed

       inadequate by the Department to handle the projected processing requirements. Department staff

       indicated that the installation of the IBM ES9021/900 was initially on a trial basis.       The

       installation of the IBM ES9021/900 CPU was done pursuant to terms contained in correspondence

       between the Department and the vendor (i.e., IBM), dated July 28 and 31, 1992.           In this

       correspondence the Department notified IBM that all aspects of the "potential purchase" were

       subject to review by the Information Resources Commission, the Department of Management

       Services, and the State Comptroller. In addition, Section 287.073(5), Florida Statutes, provides

       that the Information Technology Resource Procurement Advisory Council (ITRPAC) shall review

---

## Page 18

       and make recommendations regarding the acquisition of such data processing hardware. Section

       287.073(8), Florida Statutes, provides that the Secretary of the Department of Management

       Services shall approve or disapprove such acquisitions subsequent to the ITRPAC’s reviews and

       recommendations.




(19)                   On   December   29,   1992,   subsequent   to the   August   1992   installation of the

       ES9021/900 CPU, the ITRPAC met and recommended approval of the acquisition/ upgrade. The

       acquisition was approved by the Secretary of the Department of Management Services on the same

       date. On January 26, 1993, the Department and IBM entered into a written agreement to "clarify

       and resolve various issues arising from the contract established by the July 28 and July 31, 1992,

       correspondence (the July contract) and the computer system and services associated with that J uly

       contract."    One of the terms of this written agreement provided for the Department to pay IBM

       $9,150,000 for the ES9021/900 CPU. The State completed payment of this amount to IBM on

       February 12, 1993.




(20)                   On January 14, 1993, EDS, through its attorneys, submitted a letter to the chairman

       of the ITRPAC requesting that ITRPAC reconsider its preliminary approval of the Department's

       acquisition of the ES9021/900 CPU from IBM.          In the event ITRPAC was unable to reconsider

       its approval and recommendations regarding the acquisition, EDS provided that the letter also

       represented a petition for an administrative hearing pursuant to Section            120.57(1), Florida

       Statutes.    In this petition EDS objected to the Department's acquisition of the ES9021/900 CPU

       “premised upon use of funds intended to be paid to EDS."




(21)                  The Department’s acquisition of computer equipment from IBM is currently the

       subject of an investigation by the Office of the State Attorney of the Second Judicial Circuit.

                                        SUMMARY OF FINDINGS



(22)                Our review of the internal control structure of the   FLORIDA System indicated that

       the Department has not established and maintained policies and procedures to provide reasonable
       assurance that the System includes appropriate design features to prevent and detect material
       errors or misstatements of the data maintained within and processed by the System.



(23)                Matters coming to our attention relating to significant control deficiencies in the
       design or operation of the internal control structure for the controls reviewed are presented below.
       These control deficiencies could have a material effect on the overall evaluation of the internal
       control structure of the FLORIDA System. Paragraphs referenced and the Department's response

       thereto, exhibit E, should be read in conjunction with the items disclosed in this summary for
       a more comprehensive understanding of the findings and related recommendations.


                              System Acquisition, Design, and Implementation



         © Adequate documentation was not maintained evidencing a lack of conflicts of interest by
            all individuals participating in the evaluation and selection of the organization (i.e., EDS)
            contracted to develop and implement the FLORIDA System. (See paragraphs 29 through
            32.)



         © The Department paid EDS for a security plan and a backup and recovery plan, although
             Department staff had not indicated that the plans met the Department’s requirements. (See
             paragraphs 33 through 39.)



         © Insufficient periods of time allowed for the review of contract deliverables may have resulted
             in the Department paying for contract deliverables prior to the Department’s determination
             of the adequacy of the deliverables.    (See paragraphs 40 through 44.)

        Documentation was not available to show that amendments/modifications to
                                                                                        the contract

       for the development and implementation of the       FLORIDA System always received the

       required fiscal, contract administration, and legal staff approvals prior to their execution
                                                                                                          .
       (See paragraphs 45 through 48.)




       Contractually required benchmark/capacity testing was not adequately performed
                                                                                      .         If
       adequate tests had been timely conducted, FLORIDA System deficiencies may
                                                                                 have been
       detected and corrected in a more timely manner.      (See paragraphs 49 through 59.)



       The Department faces potential suspension and/or disallowance of Federal funds
                                                                                         for making

       Federally unauthorized extensions of food stamp client certification periods.
                                                                                        These

       unauthorized extensions were made during the development and implemen
                                                                             tation of the

       FLORIDA System.       (See paragraphs 60 through 65.)




       During the period of conversion to the FLORIDA System, the Department experience
                                                                                        d
       significant increases in errors made in determining eligibility and benefit levels for Food

       Stamp Program applicants/clients.   These increases in errors are evidenced by the increase

       in the Department’s quality control payment error rate.    These increases in errors in turn

       increase the Department’s exposure to fiscal sanctions by the Federal Government.         (See
       paragraphs 66 through 68.)




       Federally required periodic comparisons of applicant/client reported income
                                                                                       with income

       information from other State and Federal sources have not been done for those cases
                                                                                                     in

       through 73.)




       Routine automated comparisons of client records contained within the FLORIDA
                                                                                    System

       to death records maintained on the Department's computerized Bureau of Vital Statistics

       records were not done. Consequently, the Department limited its ability to ensure
                                                                                         that client

       benefits were timely adjusted or terminated upon the deaths of clients.   (See paragraphs 74

       through 77.)

                   Computer Aspects of the Internal Control Structure



User Controls



  @ The Department has not timely researched and resolved potential System problems and
    inefficiencies reported by FLORIDA System users, thereby increasing the risk that
     unauthorized services and benefits will be provided. (See paragraphs 83 through 87.)


Programmed Control Procedures



  @ Adequate computer programmed controls were not established within the FLORIDA System
     to ensure the reasonableness and completeness of data received through data exchanges.
     (See paragraphs 89 through 92.)



  @ Certain relevant information, such as the identity of persons entering and/or authorizing
     transactions, was not always contained on some of the FLORIDA System’s history files.
     Consequently, management’s capability to detect and/or determine responsibility for errors
     and irregularities was hindered.   (See paragraphs 93 through 96.)



  @ Adequate programmed edits were not established to ensure the accuracy and reasonableness
     of data entered and processed by the FLORIDA System. (See paragraphs 97 through 99.)


  © Because of the lack of adequate programmed control procedures, the Department issued
     unauthorized duplicate AFDC Program benefit payments during the process of converting
     client cases to the FLORIDA System. For the sampled month of August 1992, these
     duplicate payments may have approximated $900,000. (See paragraphs 100 through 104.)

---

## Page 22

Manual Follow-up On Computer Exception Reports



  @ The Department’s automated control established to notify Departme
                                                                      nt       employees of required

      actions in regards to public assistance program clients was not operating
                                                                                      efficiently.   The
      Department’s efforts to eliminate some of the inefficiencies through the purging
                                                                                           of data was
      not adequately controlled.    (See paragraphs 106 through 110.)



Access Controls




  ©   Access to the Department’s program documentation library was not
                                                                              adequately controlled.

      (See paragraphs 114 through 117.)



  @   Individuals, whose normal job functions included application programming,
                                                                                were assigned

      responsibility for assigning access capabilities under certain circumstances.
                                                                                        The functions

      of system security (e.g., assigning access capabilities) and application programming should

      not be performed by the same individuals.        (See paragraphs 118 and 119.)



 © Instances were noted in which employees were assigned access capabilitie
                                                                            s        that allowed them

      to perform functions outside their assigned areas of responsibility.     (See paragraphs 120

      through 122.)



Program Development



 @    An error within the FLORIDA System’s programs for determining client
                                                                                      eligibility and

      benefit levels resulted in improper adjustments to client/applicant income when
                                                                                          ascertaining

      eligibility and benefit levels for a certain category of clients/applicants.   This resulted in

      the issuance of benefits at inappropriate levels.    (See paragraphs 124 through 127.)

        © Subsequent to the close of our field work, the Department determined that due to

           programming errors within the FLORIDA System, individuals no longer eligible for
           Medicaid Program benefits were not timely removed from the records of eligible clients
           maintained within the Florida Medicaid Management Information System. The Department

           estimated that, as a consequence, approximately $28 million in Medicaid Program benefits
           have been improperly provided to ineligible individuals. (See paragraphs 128 through 132.)


       Program/System Changes



        @ Programming changes made during nightly processing of data were not adequately controlled
           to ensure that they were documented and timely reviewed by applicable management staff
           and/or acceptance/user groups.     (See paragraphs 134 through 138.)



           Revisions made to the FLORIDA System’s reference tables, which impact various aspects
           of the System’s data processing functions, were not always reviewed and approved by
           applicable user groups prior to their activation. (See paragraphs 139 through 143.)


       Computer Operations



        @ Adequate written agreements with other entities exchanging computerized data with the
            Department through the FLORIDA System had not been established, thereby jeopardizing
            the controlled transfer of such data.   (See paragraphs 145 through 149.)



         @ Within the FLORIDA System environment, certain employees had the capability to perform
            incompatible duties. Specifically, through the FLORIDA System public assistance specialists
            had the capabilities to initiate, process, record, and authorize transactions related to
            eligibility determinations, and also had the ability to access the related benefits. An
            individual performing each of these duties is in the position to perpetrate undetected errors
            and irregularities.   (See paragraphs 150 through 153.)

                         DETAILED FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS



(25)                The management of the Department is responsible for establishing and maintaining

       an internal control structure.   Our review of the internal control structure of the FLORIDA

       System involved obtaining an understanding of the System’s control environment and control

       policies and procedures.   The internal control structure comprises the policies and procedures

       that are designed and implemented to provide reasonable, but not absolute, assurance that specific
       entity objectives will be achieved.   Significant deficiencies in the design or operation of the

       internal control structure could adversely affect the organization’s ability to record, process,

       summarize, and report financial and programmatic data.         More specifically, within an EDP

       environment, a sound internal control structure provides reasonable, but not absolute, assurance

       that the system’s data is complete, reliable, and free from material error or misstatement.

       Because of inherent limitations in any internal control structure, errors or irregularities may

       nevertheless occur and not be detected. Also, projection of any evaluation of the internal control

       structure to future periods is subject to the risk that procedures may become inadequate because

       of changes in conditions or that the effectiveness of the design and operation of policies and
       procedures may deteriorate.



(26)                Our review of the internal control structure of the FLORIDA System indicated that

       the Department has not established and maintained a control environment or control policies and

       procedures to provide reasonable assurance that irregularities or material errors will be prevented
       and/or detected. We listed below instances of control policies and procedures that either are not
       adequately designed or not in place and control policies and procedures we recommend changing

       to enhance specific areas of the internal control structure.   Also, disclosed below are matters

       relating to the FLORIDA System’s control environment that limited the System’s ability to

       process transactions and maintain data in accordance with management’s expectations and/or
       controlling laws, administrative rules, and grantor restrictions. Some of the described deficiencies
       are categorized into computer aspects of the System's internal control structure.        The other

       deficiencies pertain to System acquisition, design, and implementation.

                             System Acquisition, Design, and Implementation



(27)                As noted in      paragraph   11,   the estimated cost to develop and implement the

       FLORIDA System exceeds $100 million.            Because of the System's significant cost and its vast

       impact on the Department’s operations, an effective control structure was necessary to
                                                                                                    provide
       reasonable assurances that:




            The methods of procuring contractual services for the design and implementation of
                                                                                                       the

            System were appropriate and in compliance with governing laws, administrative rules, and

            grantor restrictions.




           Criteria developed and used to evaluate potential contractors during the selection and award

            process were fair and reasonable; and the contract evaluation and award process was

            conducted on an independent and impartial basis.




            Payments to contractors were in accordance with contractual terms and were only for

            services rendered.



            The contract for design and implementation and amendments thereto were approved by

           authorized staff; related documents were prepared in accordance with governing laws,

           administrative rules, and grantor restrictions; and any contract modifications were in the

           best interest of the State.




           The System was established and implemented in a timely manner and the Department’s

           ongoing operations and services were not significantly hindered and/or interrupted during

           the conversion period.




           The System was designed to adequately meet the Department’s data processing needs and

           to ensure the processing of data in accordance with controlling laws, administrative rules,

           and grantor restrictions.

(28)                Our review disclosed significant deficiencies, as disclosed in the following

       paragraphs, in the Department’s control structure established for the acquisition, design, and

       implementation of the FLORIDA System.




(29)                Required documented _attestations of the lack of conflicts of interest by all

       Department employees involved in the evaluation and selection of the FLORIDA System

       contractor were not available. Furthermore, evidence was not provided to show that employees

       evaluating the submitted proposals had prepared and/or concurred with the related evaluation

       report.




(30)                As outlined in the Procurement Rules and Evaluation Procedures section of the

       FLORIDA System’s Request for Proposal (RFP), the Department established an evaluation

       committee to review and score each proposal received in response to the RFP. The evaluation

       committee was to prepare an evaluation report summarizing its findings regarding the proposals

       received and reviewed. The Department established a separate selection committee to receive

       the evaluation report and make a recommendation to the Secretary of the Department regarding

       the selection of a contractor. The Secretary was to select a contractor based on the selection

       committee’s recommendation, the evaluation committee’s evaluation report, and any other

       pertinent information.    Department regulations (HRSM 75-2, Section 3-11) provide that

       prospective members of contractor selection teams complete the Department’s standardized

       "Conflict of Interest Questionnaire."   The purpose of these questionnaires is to document the

       selection team members’ attestations that no conflicts of interest exist that would impact or

       interfere in the evaluation and selection of a contractor.



(31)                Our review of the contractor selection process disclosed that, generally, adequate

       documentation was maintained to evidence the selection of the FLORIDA System contractor was

       in accordance with controlling laws, administrative rules, Department regulations, and the RFP.

       However, we noted the following instances in which the Department did not evidence their

       compliance with those requirements:

                selection committee members had attested to their lack of conflicts of interest in regards

            @   Although our test disclosed no discrepancies between the final evaluation report and the

       impartial basis, we recommend that the Department enact appropriate corrective measures to

       members of the respective evaluation committees as a means of evidencing their concurrence with

                    Adequate reviews of deliverables submitted by EDS were not always conducted prior

(41)                As stated in paragraph 34, contractual terms allowed the Department ten working

       days to review and respond to a deliverable submitted by EDS. Specifically, within the allotted

       ten working days the Department was to grant approval, reject portions of or the entire

       deliverable, or request revisions.   In the event the Department rejected a portion or all of a

       deliverable, the Department was provided an additional five working days to review and respond

       to any revisions and changes made and submitted by EDS.         Notwithstanding that contractual

       provisions also provided the Department the right to require EDS to revise deliverables previously

       approved, adequate reviews of deliverables submitted by EDS were essential to ensure that the

       Department received and paid only for acceptable products.      We noted the following instance

       in which the Department’s review of certain deliverables encompassing the FLORIDA System's

       CSE financial management module did not ascertain significant deficiencies within that module

       prior to the Department’s approval and payment for those deliverables.

       payment occurred      prior   to the Department’s satisfaction   as to the deliverables’   adequacy.

       Specifically, correspondence dated February 4,       1991, stated that the detailed system design

       deliverable was approved even though it did not include all elements that would be necessary for

       Federal certification.   Also, correspondence dated December 31, 1990, approved the application

       programming deliverables even though the contract manager expressed reservations about

(44)                   The inadequate review by the Department may, in part, be attributable to the

       insufficient periods of time allowed by contractual provisions to review and respond to the

       deliverables.    Notwithstanding that the Department was late in responding to the described

       deliverables, it is questionable as to whether ten working days is a sufficient period for the

       Department to adequately review and evaluate deliverables as complex as design specification
                                                                                                    s

       and applications programming.       To assure the Department pays only for adequate services and

       products, we recommend that the Department conduct comprehensive reviews of services/ products

       received prior to related approval and payment.     Additionally, in future contracts of this type,

       deliverable review periods should be reflective of the related deliverables’ complexity, thereby

       allowing Department statf sufficient time to ascertain the adequacy of the deliverables prior to

       approval and payment.

(45)                Amendments/modifications to the contract for the development and implementation

       of the FLORIDA System did not always receive the required fiscal, contract administration, and

       legal staff approvals prior to their execution.




(46)                Sections 287.057 and 287.017, Florida Statutes, require each agency to establish

       a review and approval process for all contractual services contracts costing more than $20,000

       (formerly $6,000), which shall include, but not be limited to, program, financial, and legal review

       and approval. The law requires such reviews and approvals to be obtained before the contract

       is executed. This law applied to the Department’s contract with EDS as that contractual amount

       exceeded the statutory dollar threshold. In accordance with this law, the Department established

       written procedures (HRSM 75-2, Chapter 10) requiring that contracts and amendments thereto

       be reviewed and approved by appropriate financial and legal staff prior to their execution. The

       financial approval attests to the presence of budget authority to expend moneys for the

       initial/amended services; that the contract/amendment is in compliance with applicable State and

       Federal procurement rules and regulations; and that the method of payment is proper.           The

       financial staff designated by the Department to provide these attestations include the applicable

       budget or fiscal office and the applicable grants management office. The legal approval certifies

       that the contractor is properly bound by the contract, that the performance requirements are

       clearly defined, and that the Department is adequately protected.



(47)                 In our review we noted the following instances in which documentation was not

       available, contrary to Department regulations, to evidence required reviews and approvals of

       significant modifications to the Department’s FLORIDA System contract with EDS.              These

       instances included the following:



         @   Approvals by fiscal, contract administration, and legal staff for an amendment impacting

             the hardware and system configuration after the Department and EDS had signed the

             amendment.

(49)                 The   FLORIDA      System's   response    times   and   availability   did    not    meet      the

       Department’s requirements during the initial phase of the System’s operations.
                                                                                                         The flaws

       resulting in the initial inadequate response times and system unavailability may
                                                                                        have been

       identified and more timely corrections made had contractually required benchmark/capacity
                                                                                                                 tests
       been performed in a more timely manner.




(50)                 In the acquisition of a computer system,          the user/purchasing entity generally

       establishes operational and capacity specifications that the developing/implementing
                                                                                                         contractor

       must ensure the system     meets.    Operational   specifications typically include response time

       requirements, i.e., the system must respond to commands/instructions entered by system
                                                                                                               users
       within a specific time period.   Capacity specifications generally entail requirements relating to




                                                       -26-

---

## Page 35

Par.
No.


       how many transactions the system can process in a given period of time, and expectations as to

       the amount of time in a given normal working day that the system will be operational and

       available for use. Slow response times and system unavailability/instability due to malfunctions,

       or "downtime," may hinder the user entity’s ability to efficiently and effectively conduct its

       operations.



(51)                  Specifications established for the    FLORIDA System and incorporated into the initial

       contract between the Department and EDS included the following:



         @   For at least 95 percent of the time, response times were not to exceed a period ranging

             between one and eight seconds, depending on the type of processing requested by the user.

             The response times were to be measured every 15 minutes during a randomly selected day

             each month at randomly selected work stations.



         @ Within a consecutive 30-day period, the System was required to be available to users at least

             97 percent of the time during normal working hours.



(52)                   Pursuant to the Department’s records, the FLORIDA System was not meeting

       contractual specifications relating to availability/stability for two of the last three months that EDS

       participated in the implementation of the FLORIDA System.

(53)                   In addition, the Department’s records indicated that the FLORIDA System’s response

       times were not adequate.      For example, on May 13, 1992, the Department had its FLORIDA

       System users keep manual response time records. From these records the Department concluded

       that the average response time for that day was 2.8 minutes, far in excess of the one to eight

       seconds specified in contractual terms.

(54)                  The FLORIDA System’s initial slow response times and system unavailability were

       attributable, in part, to an unanticipated increase in the case load.        For example, when the

       Department issued the RFP, it underestimated its 1993 case load. The RFP       of May 1988 required

       that the FLORIDA System be able to handle the processing associated with a case load of 265,546

       plus 20 percent, or 318,655, Food Stamp Program cases in the year 1992-93. However, for the

       year 1991-92, the number of cases increased from 427,066 to 547,159, far surpassing the

       projections in the RFP.    Department staff indicated that the downturn in the State’s economy,

       the unauthorized extension of case certifications to receive benefits (discussed in paragraphs 60

       through 65), and the outreach effort built into the FLORIDA System all contributed to the

       escalating case load.




(55)                  The System’s initial slow response times and System unavailability were, however,

       also attributable to inadequacies and inefficiencies in the System’s initial hardware, software, and

       application   programming design.      Specifically,   for purposes of meeting data processing

       requirements, EDS installed two mainframe computers. The communications between these two

       computers used a large percentage of the available processing time, thereby limiting the System's

       overall response times.    Application design flaws included the retention of large amounts of

       obsolete data which resulted in slower response times and abnormal system downtime when

       efforts were made to exceed the data base’s record storage capacity. The described system flaws

       were identified by an independent contractor (Advanced Computer Services) during an analysis

       conducted on behalf of the Department in July and           August   1992,    after EDS ceased   its

       participation in the implementation of the FLORIDA System.




(56)                  Notwithstanding that such system flaws may be expected to surface during the period

       of system development and implementation, these flaws may have surfaced earlier and more

       timely corrections made had contractually required benchmark/capacity tests been performed by

       and/or on behalf of the Department in a more timely manner. Benchmark/capacity tests are used

       to ascertain whether a system can satisfactorily process/handle the projected case load and to

       identify system problems for correction.   As part of its contract with the Department, EDS was

       required to perform, or provide periodic data necessary to allow the Department to perform, such

       tests.   We noted that except for benchmark/capacity data submitted by EDS to the Department


                                                      -28-

---

## Page 37

Par.
No.


       in April 1992, no benchmark/capacity tests were conducted by EDS or the Department prior to

       May 31, 1992, the date EDS ceased its participation in implementation of the FLORIDA System.



(57)                During our review we observed various correspondence between the Department

       and EDS, covering the period January 30, 1991, through May 12, 1992, in which the Department

       requested data necessary for the conduct of benchmark/capacity tests. However, based on this
       correspondence, the Department was not satisfied that the appropriate data had been provided.

       Furthermore, in a signed memorandum of understanding between the Department and EDS, dated

       October 4, 1991, the Department modified contractual provisions requiring EDS to provide data

       for and/or perform benchmark/capacity testing. Specifically, the memorandum established a basis

       for a benchmark/capacity test to be conducted after additional hardware upgrades were made by

       EDS’ subcontractor (i.e., IBM). Yet, the memorandum also released EDS from the contractual

       requirements to provide evidence of successful completion of benchmark/capacity tests relating

       to the initial stages of System implementation, which had already occurred; and provided that

       the Department would not assess liquidated damages against EDS for any delays associated with

       the completion of a benchmark test.    Similarly, this memorandum of understanding is alleged

       by EDS to have released EDS from having to establish a system that met the response times as

       contained in the initial contract. The memorandum of agreement provided that the Department

       and EDS would work together to develop revised response time requirements. Revised response

       times were not, however, developed and agreed upon prior to EDS’ termination, on May 31,
       1992, of its participation in the FLORIDA System design and implementation. The stated purpose

       of the memorandum was to establish a basis for the timely completion of certain tasks relating
       to implementation of the FLORIDA System and to provide the amicable termination of the
       contractual relationship between the Department and EDS. Notwithstanding these reasons, the
       legal effect of this modification is unknown and is currently the subject of litigation as noted in

       paragraphs 15 through 17.



(58)                Slow system response times and system unavailability/instability have delayed the

       Department’s process of converting client cases from its former computerized systems to the

       FLORIDA System. These delays have in turn delayed the Department in obtaining the Federal

       certifications necessary to maximize the share of FLORIDA System costs funded with Federal

(59)                 Analyses conducted by the Department’s independent consultant and Department

       records indicate that significant improvements have subsequently been made that enhanced the

       FLORIDA System response times and availability.                  Notwithstanding these improvements, in

       future system acquisitions or significant system upgrades, we recommend that the Department

       ensure   periodic   and   timely   system    capacity    testing   is   performed.    In   addition,   contract

       specifications relating to system capacity and response times should be clearly defined and

       enforced and any modifications thereto made only when clearly in the best interests of the State

       and the respective parties to the contract.           Furthermore, to reduce the risk of litigation and

       otherwise adversarial situations detrimental to the State and other applicable parties, contractual

       terms and provisions should clearly delineate the specific responsibilities of the respective
                                                                                                               parties

       and provide for adequate documentation to determine compliance therewith.




(60)                 During the time of the FLORIDA System’s development and implementation, the

       Department    extended     certification    periods     for   participating   food   stamp   clients   without

       authorization from the Federal Government.               Consequently, the Department faces potential

       suspension and/or disallowance of Federal funds.




(61)                 One of the Department’s programs which the                FLORIDA System is designed to serve

       is the Food Stamp Program. Title 7, Section 271.4(a)(1), Code of Federal Regulations, delegates

       responsibility for client certification to the states. Certification is the process ofdetermining client


                                                             -30-

---

## Page 39

       eligibility for benefits for a given period of time, known as the certification period.      The length

       of the certification period is contingent upon factors such as the composition of the household

       and the apparent stability of its situation (e.g., a retired person with no income would be certified

       for a longer period of time than a family with a wage earner temporarily unemployed or

       disabled).   Title 7, Section 273.14, Code of Federal Regulations, provides that if clients wish

       to continue receiving benefits after the expiration of their certification period, they must be

       recertified eligible by Department staff. If the client is determined to be ineligible at that time,

       food stamp issuance to the client should cease.




(62)                 On January 29, 1992, the United States Department of Agriculture’s (USDA) Food

       and Nutrition Service (FNS) submitted to the Department a preliminary warning of possible

       suspension of Federal funding. The warning was based on an FNS investigation of the FLORIDA

       system which determined that the Department was extending certification periods of existing

       clients without Federal authority. In these instances, rather than recertifying client eligibility in

       accordance with controlling Federal regulations, the Department extended the clients’ existing

       certification periods. This, in essence, postponed the recertification of eligibility, but at the same

       time allowed the clients to continue receiving food stamps.         Under these circumstances, the risk

       of issuing food stamps to ineligible individuals increases. As described in paragraphs 66 through

       68, these unauthorized extensions of certification periods contributed to a significant increase in

       the Department’s Food Stamp Program quality control payment error rate.




(63)                 In addition   to attributing the      Federally   unauthorized extensions of existing

       Department     responded    that   certification   periods   were   extended   (i.e.,   without   Federal

       authorization) in many cases because of efforts to timely implement the FLORIDA System.

       Plan (CAP) to the FNS on March 10, 1992.              However, the FNS’s subsequent review of the

       Department’s corrective actions evidenced that the Department had not adhered to its CAP. As

       a result, on July 17, 1992, the Department received a letter from the FNS that constituted a

       formal warning of possible suspension and/or disallowance of Federal funds.              The amount of

(66)                 Significant increases in the Department’s Food Stamp Program quality control

       payment error     rate occurred   during   the   period   of FLORIDA   System    conversion,   thereby

       increasing the Department’s exposure to sanctions by the Federal Governm
                                                                               ent.



(67)                 In accordance with Title 7,        Sections 275.1   and 275.10-14,    Code of Federal

       Regulations, the Department operates a quality control system to monitor
                                                                                issuances of food

       stamps.   Staff of the Department's Quality Control units select and review samples
                                                                                               of food stamp

       cases to ascertain the accuracy and validity of the Department's eligibility and
                                                                                        benefit level

       determinations.   Errors determined by these reviews include the improper issuance or denial of

       food stamps or the issuance of food stamps in incorrect amounts.       Title 7, Section 275.23(4)(i),

       Code of Federal Regulations, establishes the authority of the USDA, Food and
                                                                                           Nutrition Service

       (FNS) to establish target quality control payment error rates relating to food
                                                                                      stamp issuances.

       Sanctions may be applied by the FNS in the event the State’s food stamp payment error
                                                                                             rate
       exceeds the target error rate.   For the 1990-91 Federal fiscal year, the Department’s final quality

       control payment error rate, as adjusted by the FNS, was 10.89 percent.
                                                                                       As this rate exceeded

       the target error rate of 10.31 percent, the FNS notified the Department that
                                                                                    it faces a potential

       sanction of $5,019,100.   Furthermore, the Department has determined its preliminary payment

---

## Page 41

       error rate for the months of October 1991 through February 1992 to be 19.27 percent. This error

       rate is almost twice the 1990-91 fiscal year error rate. These increases in the Department’s error

       rates occurred during the period of conversion to the FLORIDA System.            The Department

       attributed these increases to the following:



         @    Increased workloads resulting from increases in the number of program participants,



         ®@   Increased workloads resulting from conversion (i.e., to the FLORIDA System) and

              eligibility recertification efforts,



         @    Employees’ initial unfamiliarity with the FLORIDA System, and



         @    Programming errors within the FLORIDA System.



(68)                  The significant increases in the Department's error rate increases the likelihood of
       sanctions being imposed by the FNS. To reduce the potential for such sanctions, we recommend

       that the Department implement corrective actions that will reduce its quality control payment error

       rates to acceptable levels.



(69)                   For those cases not yet fully converted to the FLORIDA System, the Department

       has not been making Federally required periodic comparisons of applicant/client reported income
       with income information from other State and Federal sources.



(70)                   Income is one of the elements that impacts the eligibility and benefit levels of
       applicants/clients for public assistance benefits. Controlling Federal regulations require that the

       income reported to the Department by the applicants/clients be verified and documented as part
       of the eligibility determination and redetermination processes.      One Federal requirement for

       income verification is the periodic matching of applicant/client reported income with other sources

       of income information maintained by the State and Federal Governments. These other sources

       include, for example, the Internal Revenue Service and the Florida Department
                                                                                     of Labor and

                                                                               such that income

                                                                                                ed
       tapes.   The FLORIDA       System   compares   that information    with   amounts    reported   by    the

       applicants/clients as entered   into the System       by eligibility determination   workers.        Any

       discrepancies are to be resolved before eligibility and/or benefit levels are determine
                                                                                              d.

                                                                                           its

       and made comparisons with that reported by the clients/applicants.

                                                                                                the priority

(73)                Without periodic comparisons to other sources of income information, the risk of

       incorrect eligibility and/or benefit level determinations is increased.    We recommend that the

       Department continue its efforts to convert all cases from the TIF to the FLORIDA System.        In

       addition, until such conversion is complete, the Department should implement procedures

       providing for the required income comparisons for those cases maintained on the TIF.




(74)                The Department does not make routine automated comparisons of its client records

       as contained within the FLORIDA System to death records maintained on its computerized Bureau

       of Vital Statistics records, thereby limiting its ability to ensure that client benefits are timely

       adjusted upon the deaths of clients.




(75)                Termination and/or adjustment of program benefits are often necessary upon the

       death of an individual client or a member of a household unit participating as a client.     Under

       existing procedures, the Department relies upon notification by the client, client’s family, other

       individual, or the Federal Social Security Administration (SSA) (i.e., for those clients also

       receiving SSA benefits) of such deaths.       In the event notifications are not timely provided,

       unadjusted benefits may continue to be paid subsequent to clients’ deaths, contrary to controlling

       laws and grantor restrictions. In audit reports No. 11619, dated April 23, 1991, and No. 11178,

       dated March |, 1989, we described instances in which the Department continued to generate and

       issue benefit warrants subsequent to the applicable clients’ deaths.      In paragraph 128 of audit

       report No.   11619, we recommended that the Department evaluate the feasibility of making

       periodic comparisons of its Bureau of Vital Statistics (BVS) records to its computerized systems

       that generated benefit payments.      The BVS maintains records of the deaths of State of Florida

       residents in a computerized machine-readable form. Such periodic automated comparisons would

       provide an additional means for Department staff to ensure that benefits are timely terminated

       or adjusted upon deaths of clients.




(76)                In their written response to paragraph 128 ofaudit report No. 11619, the Department

       indicated that they were proceeding with implementation of the recommended corrective action.

---

## Page 44

Par.



       However, we were not provided evidence that a study had been conducted to
                                                                                 ascertain the

       feasibility of making periodic automated comparisons of BVS records to the FLORIDA System

       records.   We also noted that no such comparisons of clients in the FLORIDA System to BVS

       death records were being done.




(77)                To provide additional assurance that program benefits are issued only to authorized

       individuals, we recommend that the Department make periodic automated comparisons of its
                                                                                                     BVS
       records to client records maintained in the FLORIDA System.        In the event such comparisons

       indicate the deaths of clients, Department staff should ensure that the related FLORIDA System

       records are adjusted such that program benefits are properly and timely terminated or adjusted.




                           Computer Aspects of the Internal Control Structure



(78)                The control environment represents the collective effect of various factors on

       establishing, enhancing, or mitigating the effectiveness of specific policies and procedures.

       Within an entity or organization, a positive control environment helps assure that adequate control

       procedures are designed and placed in operation.      In regards to automated data processing, a

       positive control environment should include, among other things, the following:




         © Management involvement in setting policies for developing, modifying, and using computer

             programs and data;




         @   Establishment of an adequate organizational structure for data processing;




         ©   Appropriate methods for assigning authority and responsibility over computer systems

             documentation, including procedures for authorizing transactions and approving system

             changes;



         ©   Appropriate methods of establishing and monitoring policies for the development,

             modification, and use of computer programs and data files:

---

## Page 45

         e@   Establishment of adequate personnel policies and procedures; and



         @    Involvement of internal audit staff when appropriate.



(79)                  Control procedures represent those policies and procedures, in addition to the control

       environment, that management has established to provide reasonable assurance that specific entity

       objectives will be achieved.    Control procedures pertain to:



         @    Proper authorization of transactions and activities;



         @    Segregation of duties to reduce the opportunities for any person to be in a position to both

              perpetrate and conceal errors or irregularities;



         ®@   Design and use of adequate documents and records;



         @    Adequate safeguards over access to and use of assets and records; and



         @    Independent checks on performance and proper valuation of recorded amounts.



(80)                  Specific control procedures within an automated data processing environment, such

       as the FLORIDA System environment, may be categorized into the following types:



         @     User Controls - These controls test the completeness and accuracy of

              computer-processed transactions.



         @ Computer Application Control Procedures - This category includes two types of controls.

              The first type is programmed control procedures, which entail specific computer applications

              and are embedded in the computer programs used to operate the data processing system and

              process transactions/data.   The second type is termed manual follow-up on computer

              exception reports and entails employee investigation, correction, and/or resolution of items

              listed on computer-generated exception reports.

                                                        37.

---

## Page 46

          ©   Computer General Control Procedures - These procedures control, among other things,

              program and systems development, program and system changes, computer operations
                                                                                                        , and

              access to programs and data.




(81)                 Our review of the FLORIDA System disclosed an inadequate control environment

       that resulted in deficiencies in the design and implementation of specific control procedures
                                                                                                    . The
       inadequate control environment was attributable, in part, to lack of adequate supervision
                                                                                                 and
       monitoring    by   Department   management    during   the   various   stages   of development    and
       implementation of the FLORIDA System. The resulting deficiencies, described in the following

       and categorized by control procedure type, impaired the Department’s ability to ensure
                                                                                                that public

       funds were expended efficiently and only for authorized purposes.



       User Controls



(82)                 As described in paragraph 80, user controls test the completeness and accuracy
                                                                                                    of

       computer-processed transactions.      Such controls represent manual checks of the completeness

       and accuracy of computer output against source documentation,           One example would be the

       review, by an employee within the Department's public assistance section, of benefit
                                                                                            payment

       reports printed and generated by the FLORIDA System for the purpose of ascertainin
                                                                                          g the

       accuracy and validity of the System’s eligibility and benefit level determinations.   Problems and

       exceptions identified by users should be researched and resolved in a timel y manner to limit the

       adverse effects incorrect/improper computer programs have on an entity’s data
                                                                                     processing
       function.   Our review disclosed the following deficiency relative to research and resolution of

       potential problems identified by the FLORIDA System’s users.




                                                      ~38-

---

## Page 47

                    Potential System problems and inefficiencies reported by FLORIDA System users

       have not been researched and resolved in a timely manner.          Consequently, the risk of the

       Department providing inappropriate and/or unauthorized services and benefits is increased.




(84)                An adequate internal control structure provides for ongoing management supervision

       to determine that an automated accounting/processing system is operating as intended and is

       modified as appropriate for changed conditions. To operate efficiently, such determinations and

       any resulting modifications should be made and implemented, respectively, in a timely manner.

       The Department has established a process whereby users of the FLORIDA System who discover

       potential System problems and/or inefficiencies may submit requests to the Department's Office

       of Management Systems.      The requests are assigned to application programmers who analyze

       the circumstances and, if appropriate, provide suggested system/program modifications to correct

       the problems/inefficiencies. If the modifications are approved, the revised programs are placed

       into operation.




(85)                We selected for review 180 potential problems reported by users of the FLORIDA

       System to ascertain if they were being researched and resolved in a timely manner. Of the 180

       selected issues, 126 related to the Public Assistance (PA) component and 54 pertained to the Child

       Support Enforcement (CSE) program component.           Many of the problems selected were deemed

       to have potentially adverse effects on the proper generation of benefits by the   FLORIDA System.

       For example, some of the potential problems related to eligibility and benefit level determinations.



(86)                As shown below, our review disclosed significant delays in the research and

       resolution of the potential problems and inefficiencies identified by the System’s users:



         @   Of the 126 PA component issues, the Department’s records documented that 74, or

             approximately 59 percent, had not been resolved although the potential problems had been

             reported to the Office of Management Systems for periods ranging from 3 to 15 months.

             Of these 74 issues, the Department’s records indicated that as of the date of our review in

             September 1992, no action had been taken on 27 issues for approximately three months.




                                                       -39-

---

## Page 48

          @   Of the 54 CSE component issues, the Department's records documented that
                                                                                       29, or

              approximately 54 percent, had not been resolved although the potential problems had
                                                                                                                 been
              reported to the Office of Management Systems for periods ranging from 3 to 16
                                                                                            months.

              Of these 29 issues, the Department’s records indicate that, as of the date of our review
                                                                                                       in

              September 1992, no action had been taken on 21 issues for approximately three months.




(87)                 We recognize that some problems may require extended periods                          to resolve,

       depending on the complexity of the problem and the resources necessary for research/re
                                                                                                             solution.

       However, significant delays in research and resolution of potential problems, as disclosed
                                                                                                                in the
       Department’s records,     increase the risks of inappropriate and/or unauthorized services and

       benefits.   We recommend that the Department analyze the reasons for the excessive delays in

       resolving potential system problems and develop appropriate corrective actions providing
                                                                                                             for more

       timely research and resolution.



       Programmed Control Procedures




(88)                 As noted in paragraph 80, one type of computer application control procedure is

       programmed control procedures.        These controls pertain to specific computer applications and

       are embedded in the computer programs used in the data processing system.               These include, for

       example,    automated   controls,   or edit   checks,   that   are   designed   to   verify   the    accuracy,

       reasonableness, validity, and completeness of data/transactions entered into the system.
                                                                                                                  For

       example, one control embedded within a computer system might establish minimum and maximum

       dollar amounts that the system will accept for the processing of transactions and/or generation

       of payroll/benefit warrants.    Another type of programmed control procedures would be an

       automated check by the system that verifies all data received on a computerized tape is entered
       into the system.   In our review we noted deficiencies regarding the Department's establishment

       and implementation of adequate programmed control procedures. These deficiencies are described

       below.

---

## Page 49

                     The Department had not established adequate computer programmed controls within

        its FLORIDA System to ensure the reasonableness and completeness of data received through

        data exchanges.




(90)                  As described in paragraphs 145 through 149, the Department’s FLORIDA System

        exchanges data with other automated information systems.         To ensure the accuracy and

        completeness of information transmitted through such automated data exchanges with other

        computer systems, appropriate computer-programmed controls should be established that provide

        independent checks on the reasonableness and completeness of the data exchanged. Such controls

        include, for example, checks on record counts and/or values which provide assurance that all

        records have been received/entered and the values of received/entered transactions are accurate

        and/or reasonable.     Another computer-programmed control is the use of hash totals which,

        through the automated summation of numerical data being transmitted, provides assurance that

        only the correct information is transmitted and entered into the receiving system,



(91)                  We noted that the Department had not established adequate computer-programmed

        controls within its FLORIDA System that ensured the accuracy and completeness of exchange

        information received through computer tapes. Inquiry of Department staff disclosed that a system

        edit was performed to verify the information on data exchange tapes was loaded into the

        FLORIDA System; however, other edits to verify the validity, reasonableness, completeness,

        and accuracy of the received data were not performed.        Furthermore, record count sheets

        submitted with data exchange tapes by other entities were not used by Department staff to verify
        all records were received and loaded into the System. An environment without appropriate checks

        on the exchange of information could result in inaccurate and/or incomplete information being

        entered into the FLORIDA System and, consequently, inaccurate determinations of client

        eligibilities and/or benefit levels.




 (92)                 We recommend that the Department establish and maintain appropriate controls to

        provide reasonable assurance of the reliability and accuracy of information received through data

        exchanges. These controls should include, at a minimum, a check of input record totals against
        independently provided record counts.
