                                                                                   ns: thereby hindering

                                                                                                     of

                                                                                                  be

       upon user request.         The periodic managerial review of history screens that display
                                                                                                 individuals

       who   perform        and   authorize    selected   transactions,    as   well    as    the   dates    and    times   of
       performance/authorization, may assist in the detection of errors or irregularit
                                                                                       ies.

                                                                                         for those

(97)                Adequate programmed edits ensuring the accuracy and reasonableness of data
       entered/processed by the FLORIDA System were not established and implemented, thereby
       increasing the risk of unauthorized issuances of benefits.



(98)                Data edits represent programmed control procedures that ensure that data entered
       into a computerized system conforms with predetermined standards, thereby enabling early
       identification of potential errors. Examples include edits that ensure values entered are within
       expected parameters (limit checks) and edits that compare different data components to ensure

       their logic and consistency (reasonableness checks).    In our review of the Public Assistance

       component of the FLORIDA System, we noted, as described in the following, that adequate
       programmed edits ensuring the accuracy of data entered were not established:



         @ Because of a system error or other problem, it is sometimes appropriate to override the
            benefit amount that is automatically calculated by the System. In addition, it is sometimes
            necessary to make auxiliary payments to a client. For example, an auxiliary payment may

            be made to compensate the client for an initial miscalculation of benefits that resulted in
            an underpayment. Benefit calculation overrides and auxiliary payments are entered into the
            System to initiate the respective payment/issuance process. Notwithstanding that supervisory
            approvals of these overrides and auxiliary payments are required, the FLORIDA System
            contains inadequate edit checks to ensure the respective amounts do not exceed a reasonable
            amount. Accordingly, a circumvention of or oversight during the supervisory approval
            process may lead to the issuance of inappropriate benefits. For example, if a worker
            omitted the decimal from an amount, and the supervisor failed to detect the missing decimal,
            benefits could be inadvertently issued for a hundred times the desired amount.

           @     Data edits were not established that ensured the reasonableness and accuracy
                                                                                                  of some

                 applicant/client demographic data entered into the System. Specifically,
                                                                                          the System did not

                 indicate an error when a social security number issuance date preceded the applican
                                                                                                     t’s date
                 of birth.   Similarly, the System did not indicate an error when documentation of a
                                                                                                         female
                 applicant’s identity was listed as a "draft card."   Because females are not eligible for the

                 draft, draft cards would be an invalid form of identification in these instances.
                                                                                                     Incorrect
                 demographic data may impact the services provided and/or benefit amounts
                                                                                                 paid.



 (99)                    The lack of adequate data editing and validation during the data entry process

        increases the risk of services and benefits being provided/paid based on incorrect
                                                                                                 information.
         We recommend that, to the extent resources allow, the Department establish
                                                                                    appropriate data

        edits.    Ata minimum, these should include a reasonableness check that serves to adequatel
                                                                                                         y limit
        the amount of benefit calculation overrides and auxiliary payments.




(100)                    Adequate controls were not established during the process of converting client
                                                                                                          cases

        to the FLORIDA System.          Asa   result, the Department issued duplicate AFDC Program benefit

        Payments to clients. For the sampled month of August 1992, these duplicate payments may have

        approximated $900,000.



(101)                    During the process of converting from an existing to a new processing system,
                                                                                                              it
        is essential that controls be established to ensure that unnecessary duplication
                                                                                         of processing        is
        not performed.       Such controls become more crucial when the systems process and/or generate

        disbursements, such as benefit warrants.           Contrary to an adequate control structure and as

        described in the following, the Department issued duplicate AFDC Program
                                                                                 benefit warrants

        during its conversion to the FLORIDA System.



(102)                   As part of our review we ascertained the number of potential duplicate
                                                                                               payments

        issued during the month of August          1992.     A_ potential duplicate payment in our analysis

        represented an instance in which two benefit payments for a specific
                                                                             assistance group were

        documented as being paid during August 1992; one generated through the FLORIDA
                                                                                       System and

        the other one generated through the Transitional Issuance File (TIF).         The TIF represents the

                                                            -44-

        temporary file on which cases were maintained for processing until conversion to the FLORIDA

        System (i.e., for those cases) was completed. Our analysis showed that during the sampled month

        the   Department   issued   3,326   potential   duplicate   AFDC   Program   payments,   valued   at

        approximately $900,000.      Our extended analysis of 60 of these potential duplicate payments

        disclosed the following:



          @   Forty of the cases represented duplicate payments in which the warrants issued from the

              FLORIDA System and the TIF were both paid and redeemed by the applicant assistance

              groups; thereby approximately doubling the benefits those groups were entitled to receive.



          @   In 15 of the cases, the warrants from the     FLORIDA System had been issued and redeemed

              by the client; however, the warrants from the TIF were outstanding or canceled.



          @   In 3 of the cases, the warrants from the TIF had been issued and redeemed by the client;

              however, the warrants from the FLORIDA System were outstanding or canceled.



          @   In 2 of the cases, neither the warrants from the FLORIDA System or the TIF had been

              redeemed by the client.   In one of those two instances the warrant from the FLORIDA

              System had been canceled.



(103)                 In response to our inquiry, the Department attributed these unauthorized duplicate

        payments to the following:



          @   Department staff used the wrong transaction code when converting a case from the TIF to

              the FLORIDA System; consequently, the case was not closed on the TIF when opened in

              the FLORIDA System.




          @ Department staff used the correct transaction code during conversion, but did not make the

              data entry necessary to delete the case from the TIF.

(104)                The   Department     indicated   that   corrective       actions   were     being    implemented.

        Specifically, a review was being performed to close cases on the TIF that were currently open

        in the FLORIDA System; and a computer program was being developed to preclude the

        generation of duplicate payments for the same case in the TIF and the FLORIDA System.

        Furthermore, the Department indicated that the 3,326 potential duplicate payments were all being

        referred to the appropriate      Benefit   Recovery     Units    for overpayment determinations and

        recoupment efforts. We recommend that the Department continue their efforts to preclude future

        duplicate payments and recover funds from applicable clients in those instances where duplicate

        payments are determined     to have occurred.           Furthermore,      in    future   system   acquisitions,

        appropriate controls should be established to preclude any inappropriate duplicate processing of

        data and/or unauthorized issuance of benefits.




        Manual Follow-up On Computer Exception Reports



(105)                A second type of application control procedure consists of manual follow-up on

        computer exception reports.      These controls involve employee follow-up of items listed on

        computer exception    reports.     For example,      within     the   Department's       FLORIDA       System,

        programmed controls generate system "alerts" which indicate to the applicable employee that

        specific actions (or follow-ups) must be taken in order to ensure benefits continue to be properly

        generated and issued only to authorized clients.     Our review disclosed the following deficiencies

        regarding the Department’s process for researching and resolving (i.e., taking appropriate action

        on) alerts generated by the FLORIDA System.

(106)                The FLORIDA System’s “alert" generation process, used to notify case workers of

        required actions, was not operating efficiently as evidenced by the mass accumulation of undeleted

        alerts, thereby limiting the Department’s assurances that its programs were administered in

        accordance with controlling laws, administrative rules, and grantor restrictions.      Furthermore,

        applicable user group review and approval of the Department’s mass purges of the undeleted alerts

        was not adequately documented, thereby limiting the Department’s assurance that the correct

        deletions were made.



(107)                The FLORIDA System generates user "alerts" to notify case workers of required

        actions or to provide information. For example, one alert may notify the case worker that a child

        within a household receiving benefits is turning 18 years of age and, accordingly, the case worker

        must adjust that household’s benefit level.     Some alerts, such as those that pertain to benefit

        issuance or account delinquencies, advance to the screen of the case worker’s supervisor if the
        required action is not taken within a specified period. In the event the supervisors do not ensure

        the required actions are taken, the alerts may advance to the next higher supervisory level. Some

        alerts are not automatically deleted when the required action is completed; rather, case workers
        must make separate entries into the System to eliminate the alerts. Not promptly addressing the

        alerts increases the risk that appropriate action concerning services provided and/or eligibility
        for benefits will not be taken and, thereby, limits the Department’s assurance that its programs

        are administered in accordance with controlling laws, administrative rules, and grantor

        restrictions. In addition, the failure to take appropriate case actions, and/or to delete alerts after
        undertaking such actions, may result in the accumulation of undeleted alerts within the System,

        thereby consuming valuable computer resources and reducing the System’s processing and

        response times.



(108)                 The volume of alerts generated and accumulated within the FLORIDA System were

        indicative to the Department that the alert mechanism was not operating efficiently. Specifically,
        as of July 1992, in excess of 2.6 million alerts had accumulated within the System without
        deletion. The Department attributed the accumulation of alerts to (1) case workers not responding
        to and/or deleting alerts in a timely manner and (2) duplicate alerts.     Duplicate alerts represent

        the reproduction of existing alerts that have not been timely acted upon and removed from the

                                                         -47-

---

## Page 56

 Par.
 No.


        System.    Because the retention of excessive undeleted alerts in the System uses computer

        resources and results in slower System response times, the Departme
                                                                            nt purged approximately

         1,477,090 undeleted alerts on July 19, 1992, and 879,974 undeleted alerts on
                                                                                      August 27, 1992.



(109)                 The primary criterion for the purge was the age of the alert.        All alerts 45 days old

        or more were purged, while certain types of alerts were purged if they
                                                                                    were 14 days old or more.

        Our review of the Department’s mass purge of these undeleted alerts in J uly
                                                                                               and August 1992

        disclosed inadequate controls to ensure that only the correct data was deleted. Specifica
                                                                                                  lly, there
        was no documentation of applicable user group approval to delete the data.
                                                                                              Furthermore, there

        was no evidence that the results of the purge processing were reviewed by
                                                                                          applicable user groups.

        These circumstances increased the likelihood of the incorrect data being eliminated
                                                                                            and/or the

        failure to purge the intended alerts.



(110)                We recommend that the Department            implement    measures to ensure that any

        subsequent mass changes of data, such as the purge of undeleted alerts in July
                                                                                               and August 1992,

        be undertaken only after documented approval of the planned changes by users
                                                                                     as well as the
        Department’s electronic data processing staff.      Documentation should also be maintained to

        evidence approval by the affected user groups of the results of such changes.
                                                                                                        We also
        recommend that the Department continue their efforts to examine the alert
                                                                                  generation             process

        for the purpose of ensuring timely case actions, while at the same time maximizing computer

        resources and related response times.




        Computer General Control Procedures



(111)                General   control   procedures   increase   the   assurance   that    programmed    control

        procedures operate effectively. General control procedures control, among other things,
                                                                                                       computer

        program development, changes to computer programs, computer operation
                                                                              s, and access to

        computer programs and system data.

---

## Page 57

                      Access Controls.   Computer general contro] procedures over access to programs

        and data are intended to prevent or detect unauthorized changes to computer programs and to

        data files.   Proper access controls will assist in the prevention or detection of deliberate or

        accidental errors caused by improper use or manipulation of data files, unauthorized or incorrect

        use of a computer program, and/or improper use of computer resources. Access controls include

        physical security devices and techniques such as guard services, door locks, and secure storage

        devices. Other access controls include software that (1) limits programmer access to production

        programs and live data files; (2) operator access to source code and individual elements of data

        files; and (3) user access to defined programs and data files.     Examples of software controls

        include the use of passwords and software packages that (1) limit access to programs and data

        files, (2) log violations of programmed access, and (3) maintain profiles of users and privileges

        and changes to users and such privileges.



(113)                 Our review disclosed significant deficiencies in the Department’s control structure

        relative to controlling access to the FLORIDA System documentation and data files.           These

        deficiencies are described in the following paragraphs.



(114)                 Access to the Department’s program documentation library was not adequately

        controlled.



(115)                 Access to system design specifications, program documentation, program change

        documentation, and other documentation relating to a system or group of systems is often

        controlled through the librarian function.    In this function one or more persons, or librarians,

        are assigned the responsibility of maintaining access to a physical location, or "library," in which

        this documentation is stored.



(116)                 In regards to the FLORIDA System, the program documentation library consists

        of a single room secured by a lockable door. Keys to the door are maintained by the assigned

        librarian and other authorized individuals.   Individuals accessing the library are allowed to




                                                        -49-

---

## Page 58

 Par.



        examine    the    program    documentation   within    the   library   and/or       to   remove   the   program

        documentation for further review. Numerous tracking systems have been used by the Departmen
                                                                                                    t
        to maintain control of document utilization.



(117)                    Although the librarian usually locks the door to the library when she leaves the

        room, during our review we observed instances where the door was left unlocked and
                                                                                                                the room

        unattended.      Under these circumstances, unauthorized access to program documentation may

        occur by unauthorized individuals accessing the library. Such unauthorized access increases the

        risk of unauthorized modifications to programs, which in turn increases the risk of undetected

        errors and irregularities.    Furthermore, responsibility for resulting errors and irregularities may

        be difficult, if not impossible, to determine.        We recommend that the Department implement

        corrective measures providing for controlled access to the library. These measures should include

        ensuring that the library is locked when unattended by the librarian.



(118)                 Contrary to_an adequate segregation of employee duties, employees normally

        responsible for application programming were assigned to function as backups to one of the

        Department’s application security administrators.




(119)                 Within the FLORIDA System, the application security administrators are responsible

        for controlling employee access to the FLORIDA System.                   Their duties include receiving,

        reviewing, and approving requests by employees to obtain appropriate user identification numbers

        and passwords allowing them to access and/or process certain information through the FLORIDA

        System. Our review disclosed that two of the three employees assigned to perform the functions

        of the FLORIDA System application security administrator at Department Headquarters, when

        that individual was not available, were employed as project analysts whose duties included

        application   programming.       Since   individuals   serving   in    the   role    of application     security

        administrator are in the position to assign access and processing capabilities to themselves, these

        individuals would have the ability to make unauthorized changes to data files using applications

        which they programmed.        An individual performing these incompatible duties is in the position




                                                         -5()-

---

## Page 59

Par.



        to perpetrate errors or irregularities without detection in a timely manner. We recommend that

        the Department assign individuals not responsible for application programming to serve as

        application security administrators.



(120)                Instances were noted in which employees were assigned access capabilities that

        allowed them to perform functions outside their assigned areas of responsibility.



(121)                Within the FLORIDA System, user profiles were established to control access to

        data and transactions.    A user profile represents a series of access capabilities designated for a

        particular group of employees with similar access/data processing needs.         Different profiles,

        providing different access capabilities, are assigned to different groups of users.   For example,

        employees who determine applicant eligibility for program benefits may be assigned a profile

        that allows access to various data and programs necessary to make eligibility determinations.

        Application programmers, however, would be assigned a separate profile that would allow them

        to read data, but not to change or process it.    An adequate control structure requires that user

        profiles provide access no greater than what is required for the duties of those persons assigned

        those profiles.   Our tests of user profiles assigned to 12 selected employees disclosed the

        following deficiencies:



          @ The user profile established for six State security administrators allowed update capabilities

             to certain aspects of case/client record management.      For example, this profile allows the

             user to record in the FLORIDA System a request for administrative hearings to determine

             whether disqualification of client benefits is appropriate. The profile also allows the user

             to record in the FLORIDA System actions taken relative to the hearings. These actions may

              include informing a client of the results of a hearing, reduction of benefits, or changes in

             client classification.   Such capabilities are not necessary to the performance of the State

              security administrators’ duties.




                                                         -5|-

---

## Page 60

 Par.




           © A      system librarian was assigned the profile for a State security administrator although the

                 individual had not served in that position for a period in excess of eight months. Subsequent

                 to our inquiry, the Department corrected that individual's profile status.



(122)                    To reduce the likelihood of undetected errors or irregularities, we recommend that

        the Department establish procedures providing for routine reviews of profiles to ensure that

        employees’ access capabilities are no greater than what is needed for them to perform their

        assigned duties.



(123)                    Program Development. Computer general control procedures over the development

        of programs should ensure that applications/programs are suitably authorized, designed, and

        tested. Examples include user involvement in design and approval of programs and development

        of test data and program testing.       Our review disclosed the following deficiencies regarding the

        development of the computer programs                 implemented   to assist   Department employees       in

        ascertaining eligibility and related benefit levels for AFDC Program applicants and to terminate

        Medicaid Program benefits for individuals no longer eligible for services.



(124)                   For_a_    specific   category   of   the   Department’s   Medicaid   Program   clients,   a

        programming error within the FLORIDA System resulted in the improper adjustments to

        client/applicant_income when ascertaining eligibility and benefit levels for AFDC Program

        benefits.    This resulted in the issuance of benefits at inappropriate levels.



(125)                   As described in paragraph 6, two of the programs for which the FLORIDA System

        processes data are the AFDC and Medicaid Programs.                  The AFDC Program provides cash

        assistance to eligible family groups while the Medicaid Program provides medical benefits to

        eligible recipients.     The eligibility requirements for both programs are similar, such that family

        groups eligible for AFDC benefits are generally also eligible for Medicaid Program benefits.

        However, under certain circumstances, individuals may be determined eligible for Medicaid

        Program benefits but not for AFDC Program benefits.             Such cases are deemed "Medicaid-only"

        Cases.




                                                              {42

---

## Page 61

                     To qualify for benefits under these programs, applicants’ incomes may not exceed

        predetermined levels established by the Federal/State Government. Federal regulations, however,

        allow certain adjustments, termed disregards, to applicants’ incomes when ascertaining income
        levels for eligibility determination purposes.   One allowed adjustment is a reduction of total

        monthly earned income by $30 for the first 12 months of program eligibility.      An additional

        adjustment is a further reduction of total monthly earned income by an amount equal to one-third
        of the remaining monthly earned income. This second adjustment is only allowed for the clients’
        first four months of direct assistance.   In addition, these adjustments may only be made to

        incomes for the following:



          @ Those recipients who have not received direct assistance (i.e., AFDC benefits) for 12
             consecutive months;



          @ Medicaid-only cases that have not received AFDC or Medicaid Program benefits for 12

             consecutive months;



          @ Those recipients who have never received the disregards or never have had them applied
             for four consecutive months; and have been eligible for and received direct assistance in
             one of the past four months; or



          @ Certain recipients who have a total net income equal to or less than the applicable Federally
             established maximum allowable income level for that family group.



(127)                Some of the client records converted to the FLORIDA System tor case management

        purposes were Medicaid-only cases that had received AFDC Program benefits at some time during
        the past 12-month period. Subsequent to the applicable case conversion to the FLORIDA System,
        some of these Medicaid-only clients again became eligible for and received AFDC Program
        benefits. As these cases had received AFDC Program benefits during the past 12-month period,
        they were not eligible for the income disregards as described above.       However, contrary to

        controlling regulations, the FLORIDA System applied the income disregards to these cases,

        resulting in the issuance of benefits at inappropriate levels. The Department became aware of

---

## Page 62

        these circumstances and prepared a production problem report to commence correction of the

        problem.      To stop the issuance of inappropriate benefits, we recommend that the Department

        correct this system error as soon as possible.     In addition, for applicable cases the Department

        should determine the amount of benefits improperly issued and commence appropriate recoupment

        activities.



(128)                   Subsequent to the close ofour field work, the Department determined that individuals

        no longer eligible for Medicaid Program benefits were not timely removed from the records of

        eligible clients.



(129)                  The Department’s contracted fiscal agent for the Medicaid Program, Consultec, Inc.,

        relies on the Florida Medicaid Management Information System (FMMIS) to ensure that service

        providers (i.€., hospitals, physicians, etc.) are paid only for services rendered to eligible

        individuals.    Information relating to individuals’ eligibilities for Medicaid Program Services is

        transferred to the FMMIS from the FLORIDA System.             Subsequent to the close of our field

        work, the Department ascertained that, due to programming errors within the FLORIDA System,

        individuals no longer eligible for Medicaid Program benefits were not being timely removed from

        the records of eligible clients maintained within the FMMIS.     Asa   result, individuals no longer

        eligible for Medicaid Program services continued to receive benefits subsequent to the dates their

        eligibilities terminated.    The Department estimated that approximately 200,000 ineligible

        individuals improperly remained on the eligibility records within the FMMIS prior to the

        Department's identification of the problem. The value of Medicaid Program benefits improperly

        received by the ineligible individuals has been estimated by the Department to be approximately

        $28 million.




                                                         -54-

---

## Page 63

                     In correspondence submitted to our staff on March 19, 1993, the Department

        indicated that the programming errors resulting in the described deficiency involved a FLORIDA

        System functionality problem that resulted in some individuals improperly being retained on the

        FLORIDA System as eligible for services, although the individuals’ assistance groups had been

        closed (i.e., eligibility terminated) on the FLORIDA System.        In addition, the Department

        indicated that other programming errors resulted in the incorrect transmissions of beginning and

        ending dates for eligibility periods from the FLORIDA System to the FMMIS.



(131)                The Department indicated that in May 1992 Department staff initially became aware

        that a potential problem existed relating to the accuracy of FMMIS data.     However, due to the

        following circumstances, the Department indicated that Department staff did not become aware

        of the problem’s significance until February 1993:



          @ Initial efforts and resources were directed towards correcting verified significant problems
             such as slow response times and system instability.



          @ Temporary reassignment of technical staff members and resources due to Hurricane Andrew.


          @ Necessity of correcting the described     FLORIDA System functionality problems before an

             accurate reconciliation of the FLORIDA System and FMMIS eligibility records could be
             accomplished.



          @ A problem with the interface between the FLORIDA System and the FMMIS that hindered
             an accurate reconciliation of eligibility records.



(132)                The Department indicated that corrective action has been implemented to remove

        ineligible individuals from the records of eligible clients within the FMMIS and to ensure accurate

        transfers of data from the FLORIDA System. We recommend, in addition to actions taken, that
        the Department enact corrective measures to ensure timely and periodic reconciliations of
        eligibility records between the FLORIDA System and FMMIS. Absent such reconciliations the
        Department has little assurance that such errors will be detected on a timely basis in the future.

(133)                     Program/System Changes.     Computer general control procedures over changes to

        existing programs and systems should ensure that modifications to application
                                                                                      programs are
        properly approved, designed, tested, and implemented. The issues associated
                                                                                    with controls over

        program and system changes are similar to those relating to the developm
                                                                                ent of new programs

        and systems.        We noted the following deficiencies relative to the Department’s process for

        modifying FLORIDA System programs.




(134)                     Programming changes made during nightly processing of data were not
                                                                                              adequate   ly
        controlled to ensure that they were documented and timely reviewed by applicable manageme
                                                                                                         nt
        staff and/or acceptance/user groups.



(135)                     The primary function performed by computer programmers is the design, creation,

        modification, and revision of computer programs used to process data and transactions.
                                                                                                 As stated

        in paragraph 144, computer programmers should not have the capability to process data
                                                                                              and
        transactions using the programs which they have the capability of revising or
                                                                                      modifying.

        Furthermore, review and approval of computer program test results by the applicable
                                                                                              user groups,

        prior to the programs’ placement into operation, is essential to preclude undetected
                                                                                             errors or

        irregularities.



(136)                 During nightly processing of data and transactions, computer programs within the

        FLORIDA System are subject to problems that result in interruptions and/or improper processing.

        Accordingly, the Department assigns programmers to work night shifts on a cyclic basis.        All

        FLORIDA System applications programmers are given read access to the production
                                                                                                 libraries.

        This access allows them to copy computer programs from the production libraries, where the

        programs that normally process data reside, into a separate "fix" or "override"
                                                                                        library.    In the

        event of a problem within a program during nightly processing, the fix and override libraries

        provide a mechanism for the programmers to modify or revise the programs such
                                                                                      that nightly

        processing can be resumed.       The resumption of data processing is done using the revised

---

## Page 65

Par.
No.


        programs within the fix or override libraries.     The revised programs are not moved back into

        the production libraries until the applicable supervisor and user/acceptance group have approved

        the modifications/revisions made by the programmer. These reviews and approvals occur after

        the revised programs have resumed processing of data.




(137)                   While we recognize the importance of establishing procedures to ensure that essential

        processing of data and transactions is not interrupted for extended periods, we noted the following

        deficiencies within the Department’s implementation of those procedures that increased the risk

        of undetected errors and irregularities:



          @   Procedures were inadequate to ensure that all changes made to programs copied to the fix

              and override libraries were reviewed and approved by supervisors and acceptance/user

              groups.      Specifically, existing procedures provide for the programmer making changes to

              manually record these changes on a report.         These manually prepared reports are the

              mechanism for informing the programmers’ supervisors and applicable acceptance/user

              groups that a program has been revised and should be reviewed and approved. Under these

              circumstances, changes made by a programmer and not recorded on the manually prepared

              report may not be detected, and thus not reviewed.




          @   Data continues to be processed using the programs in the fix and override libraries until

              approval is provided by the acceptance/user group and the program transferred back to the

              production libraries.    Consequently, if changes made by a programmer are not reviewed

              in a timely manner by the applicable supervisor and acceptance/user group, the risk

              increases that improper processing of data will occur for extended periods of time.

              Furthermore, the placement of programs into the fix and override libraries for extended

              periods of time increases the possibility of unauthorized revisions by those programmers

              assigned the capability to make modifications within those libraries. We noted that changes

              made by programmers were often not reviewed and approved in a timely manner. This was

              evidenced by the lengths oftime that various programs have remained in the fix and override

              libraries.    Specifically, we noted that some elements had been in the override library for

              over a year.

(138)                  To reduce the risk of unauthorized changes and undetected errors and irregularities,

        we recommend that the Department establish the following corrective measures:




           © To the extent practicable, implement software that provides a secured record of all changes

              made by programmers to programs in the fix and override libraries.       Such records should

              not be available to the programmers making those changes and should be used by

              supervisors and acceptance/user group in reviewing and approving changes.




          ©   Implement reasonable time standards in which changes made to programs in the fix and

              override libraries must be reviewed, deleted, corrected, approved, and/or placed back into

              the production libraries.   We noted that the Department has initiated a plan to implement

              such standards.    That plan should be carried out.



(139)                  Revisions made to the FLORIDA System’s reference tables, which impact various

        aspects of the System's data processing functions, were not always reviewed and approved by

        applicable user groups prior to their activation.



(140)                  One of the efficiencies designed for and used by the   FLORIDA System is reference

        tables.   Reference tables represent an architecture of codes and parameters used to facilitate data

        processing.    Reference tables would include, for example, a set (i.e., table) of specific data

        processing dates programmed into the System.        Such a table would be automatically accessed by

        the System, based on user commands, and the dates contained therein would control when the

        System commenced specific processing of data.       For example, the generation of benefit warrants

        would automatically be initiated based on a specific date or dates contained in the reference table.

        Another use of a reference table would be to establish parameters controlling eligibility for

        benefits. For example, maximum asset and income limits could be entered into a reference table:

        then in the System’s eligibility determination processing, an automatic comparison would be made




                                                        -58-

---

## Page 67

Par.
No.


        of an applicant’s asset and income data entered by the eligibility determination worker to the

        maximum amounts in the reference table.         Reference tables could also be used to establish

        parameters for on-line error messages.       For example, if an instruction/amount entered by a

        System user violates/exceeds a parameter/amount in the established reference table, an error-

        message would be generated.



(141)                The Department assigned individuals to serve as the FLORIDA System’s reference

        table coordinators. The coordinators are to update and activate the   FLORIDA System reference

        tables based on documented/approved requests from the Department’s public assistance, CSE,

        and Office of Management Systems programming staffs.         Examples of updates would include

        changes in desired processing dates or changes in asset/income eligibility limits. Because of the
        pervasive impact reference table elements have on data processing, it is essential that the
        applicable requesting user group acknowledge the accuracy and validity of the revised/updated
        reference tables prior to their activation by the reference table coordinators.



(142)                In our tests, we noted that the Department maintained documentation evidencing that

        the public assistance staff approved changes made by the reference table coordinators prior to
        their being activated for daily operation.   However, for reference table changes relating to the

        CSE component or the programming section, documentation was not maintained evidencing
        applicable approvals by CSE or Office of Management Systems programming staffs.


(143)                On September 17, 1992, subsequent to our tests, the Department issued new standard

        operating procedures for making changes/updates to the FLORIDA System’s reference tables.

        These procedures required the requesting user group to review, prior to activation, the

        revisions/updates made by the reference table coordinators. Under these procedures, activation
        is generally not to occur until the reference table coordinators receive a memorandum from the

        requesting user group authorizing system activation.      We will review the adequacy of these

        revised procedures in our subsequent audits/reviews of the Department.

---

## Page 68

                       Computer Operations. Computer general control procedures are intended to ensure

        that application programs are properly used and that the proper data files are used during data

        processing.    Examples of such controls include records, or logs, evidencing when applicable

        programs were used and/or the occurrence of significant events such as interruptions,         and

        procedures for backing up data and program files. Other controls relative to computer operations

        include the segregation of duties such that no one employee is in the position to both perpetrate

        and conceal errors or irregularities without detection in a timely manner.       For example, an

        individual generating or modifying computer programs used in the processing of data should not

        also be allowed to process transactions through the computer using those programs he/she

        modified. Similarly, in an automated data processing environment, as ina manual data processing

        environment, the functions of initiating, processing, and authorizing transactions should be

        segregated among different employees. Our review disclosed the following deficiencies regarding

        the Department’s control structure over computer operations.



(145)                  Adequate written agreements had not been established with other entities exchanging

        computerized data with the Department through the FLORIDA System, thereby jeopardizing the

        controlled transfer of such data.




(146)                  Data exchanges are characterized by a two-way flow of data in which an entity sends

        a request to another entity and a response is received with applicable matching information from

        that entity’s computerized data files.    To facilitate the timeliness and accuracy of its data

        processing functions, the Department implemented the integrated data exchange module within

        the FLORIDA System.       This module allows the FLORIDA System to exchange data with other

        automated information systems within the Department and with other governmental and private

        entities.   For example, information may be obtained for income verification purposes, as part

        of the applicant eligibility determination process, through exchanges with automated systems

        within the Federal Internal Revenue Service and the Florida Office of the Auditor General,

        Division of Public Assistance Fraud.

(150)                 Employees,   responsible for determining applicant/client eligibility for benefits

        through the FLORIDA System, performed and/or had the capability to perform incompatibl
                                                                                               e

        duties, thereby increasing the risk of undetected errors and irregularities.



(151)                As described in paragraph 78, one of the attributes of a positive control environment

        is an adequate organizational structure.   An adequate organizational structure provides for the

        segregation of duties among different employees, such that no one individual is in the position

        to initiate, process, record, and authorize transactions and have access to the related assets.   An
        individual who performs incompatible duties is in the position to conceal errors and irregularities.



(152)                In addition to the need to segregate computer programming and operation functions

        as described in paragraph 112, the environment of the Department’s FLORIDA System mandates

        that the functions of initiating, processing, recording, authorizing, and having access to benefits

---

## Page 71

Par.
No.


       paid on behalf of the Department’s public assistance clients be segregated among different

       employees. Not segregating these incompatible duties may increase the risk of public funds being

       diverted without detection in a timely manner.         During our review, we noted the following

       instances in which duties were not always adequately segregated:



         @ After an applicant’s basic demographic information (i.e., name, date of birth, social security

            number, etc.) has been entered into the FLORIDA System by an interview clerk as part of

            the client registration process, the applicant is interviewed by a public assistance specialist

            (PAS). During the interview the PAS collects from the applicant the information necessary

            to determine eligibility for benefits. The information collected by and entered into the

            FLORIDA System by the PAS includes dependents’ names, income and asset amounts,

            etc. In addition, the PAS may revise/update the information gathered and entered into the

            System by the interview clerk, including names and addresses.       Based on the information

            entered, the FLORIDA System determines eligibility and, if applicable, performs the benefit
            calculations. For cases approved/authorized by the PAS, the applicable information within

            the FLORIDA System is extracted and used to generate warrants which are mailed to

            applicable clients at the addresses entered/approved by the PAS. PAS’s retain the capability

            to update and revise case information after the applicants’ initial authorization for benefits.
            Notwithstanding that the PAS under normal operating conditions cannot generate a fictitious

            case in the System, under the described scenario the PAS is in the position to alter an

            otherwise ineligible case through the entry of fictitious information and divert the resulting

            benefits to a fraudulently entered address. The Department's monitoring of case files and

            reviews by its Quality Control staff may subsequently detect such an unauthorized diversion,

            however, we question whether such detections would be timely.

           @ The FLORIDA System is designed to preclude individual employees from
                                                                                  both (1)            entering

              the initial client information for a case into the System and (2) instructing the
                                                                                                   System to

              perform eligibility and benefit level determinations and authorizing benefits.
                                                                                                  However,

              during the process of converting cases to the FLORIDA System, case workers
                                                                                                  (i.e., public

              assistance specialists) were allowed to enter the initial client information as contained
                                                                                                          in the
              previous data processing systems into the FLORIDA System, instruct the FLORIDA System

              to determine eligibility, and authorize the benefits.   Notwithstanding that this was done to

              facilitate the conversion process, under those circumstances benefits could have
                                                                                                     been
              diverted for unauthorized purposes without timely detection.



(153)                 To ensure that benefits are paid only to authorized individuals and in the correct

        amounts, we recommend that the Department implement appropriate
                                                                        measures to ensure that,

        to the extent practicable, individual employees do not perform incompatible duties. Specificall
                                                                                                        y,
        the functions of entering initial client information, processing the cases for eligibility and
                                                                                                         benefit
        level determinations, and approving/authorizing the cases for benefits should be segregated
                                                                                                         among
        different employees.   Additionally, revisions made by case workers to client information such

        as names, addresses, etc., should be reviewed/authorized by appropriate supervisor
                                                                                                y personnel.



(154)                The Department did not maintain or retain adequate computer logs documenting all

        significant events and actions relating to the FLORIDA System's equipment, software, data base,

        tuning activities, and operations; thereby limiting management's capability to review
                                                                                              System
        changes and identify or ensure timely resolution of System problems and inefficiencies.



(155)                Within an automated data processing environment, change and problem management

        is critical to achieve a stable, reliable, and well-controlled operation.   Essential to change and

        problem management is the recording, or logging, of significant events involving the
                                                                                                   applicable

        system’s hardware and software.     Periodic reviews of recorded, or logged, hardware/software

        acquisitions, updates, enhancements, break downs, etc., assist management, for example, in

        verifying the accuracy of billings for maintenance and/or in identifying system inefficienc
                                                                                                   ies.

        The logging of corrective actions also provides management a mechanism to ensure
                                                                                                 the problem

        resolution process is timely and appropriate.


                                                        -64-

---

## Page 73

Par.
No.


(156)                We noted that the Department did         not maintain and/or retain computer logs

        evidencing the occurrence of all significant events and actions pertaining to the FLORIDA

        System’s equipment and operations. For example, the Department’s operations log did not reflect

        the physical transfer of the computer memory between separate central processing units.

        Adequate computer logs were also not maintained that documented all significant modifications

        to the System’s software and data base products; installation of new products, or new versions

        of existing products; removal of products; and various tuning activities (i.e., changes to improve

        system/application performance).    Additionally, logs that were prepared were retained only for

        a period of approximately 60 days. Such retention periods would not always be adequate to allow

        for appropriate managerial review.




(157)                To allow for adequate change and problem management, we recommend that the

        Department establish procedures providing for the logging of all significant events relative to the

        FLORIDA System’s operations, equipment, software, data bases, and tuning activities. Retention

        periods for those logs should be adequate to allow for appropriate managerial review.



(158)                Adequate managerial reviews were not conducted of the use of the Department’s

        software established for making direct updates to the FLORIDA System’s production data.        The

        lack of such reviews increases the risk of unauthorized modifications not being detected in a

        timely manner.



(159)                Organizations with on-line applications such as the FLORIDA System sometimes

        modify data directly (i.e., make modifications outside of the control of established application

        security) because processing errors have disabled the normal on-line transactional means of

        modification. For example, omission of vital data during conversion of a case may result in the

        System’s inability to retrieve that case. The data must then be added directly, i.e., added using

        special software.   To preclude unauthorized processing and/or changes to data, access to the

        utility software that can perform such direct update to production data should be controlled,

        procedures for its use should be established, and its use should be monitored by individuals

        independent of the staff using the utility.




                                                       -65-

---

## Page 74

 Par.



(160)                 The Department’s software utility package used to make direct updates
                                                                                            to the

        FLORIDA System production data is termed "DLT/0."             Designated programmers within the

        Department’s Office of Management Systems application support section determine
                                                                                                   the need to
        use the DLT/O software utility upon review of system/programming problems communica
                                                                                                       ted by

        various users.    Approval from supervisory staff within the application support section must be

        obtained   prior to   making   modifications through   the   DLT/O software     utility.    After the
        modifications are made, the supervisory staff within the application support section
                                                                                                   review the
        results of the run.



(161)                We noted that the Department had not established adequate control over the DLT/O

        software utility package used to perform direct update to the FLORIDA System production
                                                                                                         data.
        Specifically, supervisory staff within the Department’s Office of Management Systems application

        support section had the capability to initiate, authorize, and review the running
                                                                                          of the utility.

        Reviews by    knowledgeable user groups or other knowledgeable staff independent of the

        programming section were not done.         Under these circumstances the risk is increased that

        unauthorized alterations, should they occur, to production data, such as client records,
                                                                                                   maintained

        within the FLORIDA System will not be detected in a timely manner. We recommend that the

        Department establish and implement procedures providing for managerial reviews of
                                                                                                   the DLT/O

        software’s use.   Such reviews should be conducted by knowledgeable managerial staff who are

        independent of the programmers initiating and authorizing the software’s use.



(162)                Due to limited positions and resources, the Department’s internal audit staff have

        had limited involvement in developing and/or reviewing the FLORIDA System.



(163)                Independent checks on performance are essential in an adequate control structure.

        One means of providing such independent checks is through an entity’s internal audit function.

        In addition, Section 282.318(3)(a)5., Florida Statutes,      provides that each    head of a State

        department shall ensure that periodic internal audits and evaluations of the security program for

        data and information technology resources are conducted.




                                                       -66-

---

## Page 75

                      We noted, however, that the Department’s internal audit function has had no

        significant involvement with the development of the FLORIDA System.              In addition, as of

        January 7, 1993, the Department's internal audit staff had made no review of the FLORIDA

        System’s security program.       In correspondence      with our staff, dated July 7,     1992,   the

        Department’s internal audit administrator indicated that internal audit staff involvement in the

        development and review of the FLORIDA System was desired as well as discussed by Department

        management during the initial phases of the FLORIDA System project.           However, because of

        limited staff and resources, and the lack of any electronic data processing (EDP) auditor positions,

        it was determined by Department management that internal audit staff involvement would be

        limited.   In its budget request submitted for the 1992-93 fiscal year, the Department requested,

        but did not receive, funds and authorization for 16 professional computer audit staff positions.



(165)                 To enhance the controls relative to FLORIDA System security and operating

        procedures, we recommend that, to the extent funds and EDP audit positions become available,

        the Department involve its internal audit function in        reviews of the FLORIDA System’s

        performance and security program.



                                                  Other Reviews



(166)                 During the week of December 7, 1992, the USDA’s FNS conducted a review of

        the FLORIDA System to determine the level of system performance in certain areas of

        functionality which were of concern to the FNS.        In correspondence addressed to and received

        by the Department in February 1993, the FNS indicated that FNS staff were impressed by the

        magnitude of the System’s capabilities and functions, but noted that some significant problems

        existed. These problems included, for example, inadequate controls over auxiliary issuances and

        various programming errors that impacted benefit levels and eligibility determinations. The FNS

        has requested that the Department provide a written response indicating the specific actions and

        time frames for correcting the FNS identified problems. As of March 16, 1993, the Department

        was in the process of preparing its corrective action plan for submission to the FNS.




                                                        -67-

---

## Page 76

Par.




                                              Summary Statement



(167)                 Obviously, the deficiencies described within this report demonstrate significant

        inadequacies in the Department’s internal control structure relative to the development
                                                                                                and
        implementation of an automated data processing system intended to process billions of
                                                                                                    dollars
        of program benefits.     In addition to costing the Department significant funds in terms of

        improperly paid benefits and resources necessary to remedy problems, such deficiencies
                                                                                               have
        contributed to the Department's exposure to litigation risks as evidenced by the lawsuit described

        in paragraphs 15 through 17.   Until the noted deficiencies are corrected and an adequate internal

        control structure is placed into operation, an increased risk exists that similar deficiencies
                                                                                                       will

        occur. Accordingly, we encourage the Department to implement the recommendations contained

        within this report.

                                                                    Major Activity

May 1988                                Approved FLORIDA RFP Issued.


                                        The Department received approval for the FLORIDA System RFP from
                                        the applicable Federal agencies (DHHS and FNS) and from State of Florida
                                        officials.   After placing announcements in several publications the final
                                        RFP was officially released on May 20, 1988.


December 1988                           Protest Filed Contesting the Award of the FLORIDA System Contract.


                                        The Department determined through its evaluation process that the contract
                                        should be awarded to EDS.         Once the award was made public, Unisys
                                        Corporation filed a Notice of Intent to Protest with the Department.


March 1989                              Resolution of Protest.


                                        The protest filed by Unisys Corporation was heard by the Division of
                                        Administrative Hearings.     The final order recommended that the contract
                                        be awarded to EDS.


May 1989                                Federal Approval of FLORIDA System Contract.


                                        DHHS and FNS approved the awarding of the contract to EDS.


May 1989                                FLORIDA System Contract Signed.


                                        The Department signed a contract with EDS for the development and
                                        implementation of the FLORIDA System.


June 1989                               FLORIDA System Development Begins.


                                        EDS began developing the FLORIDA System.


January 1990                            Contract Amendment 001 Signed.


                                        This amendment made several changes to the contract including an updated
                                        deliverable schedule.       It   also   changed   the   district conversion   and
                                        implementation schedule and changed the type of terminal cabinetry to be
                                        provided.

—No._            FINDING #1

(29-32)          Required documented attestations of the lack of conflicts of
                 interest by all department employees involved in the evaluation
                 and selection of the FLORIDA System contractor were not
                 available.  Furthermore, evidence was not provided to show that
                 employees evaluating the submitted proposals had prepared and/or
                 concurred with the related evaluation report.

                 RECOMMENDATION #1

                 The department enact appropriate corrective measures to ensure
                 future selection committee members complete the standardized
                 Conflict of Interest Questionnaires.  In addition, subsequent
                 evaluation reports should be prepared and/or signed by members of
                 the respective evaluation teams as a means of evidencing their
                 concurrence with the          reports'       conclusions.

                 MANAGEMENT’S RESPONSE #1

                 We concur.  Department staff will ensure, for future
  il,            procurements, evaluators sign the recommendation.



                 FINDING       #2

(33-39)          The department paid EDS for a security plan and backup and
                 recovery plan although appropriate department                 staff had not
                 authorized the plans as having met the department's requirements.

                 RECOMMENDATION #2


                 In subsequent contractual relationships, the department pay only
                 for those services and/or products that it deems to meet
                 appropriate standards.


                 MANAGEMENT’S RESPONSE #2

                 We concur.          By June 15,    1993,     Management Systems staff will
                 complete the update of the HRS                Disaster Recovery Plan and the HRS
                 Security Plan to incorporate the FLORIDA environment.   We will be
                 ready for testing the backup hot-site by July 23, 1993.   This
                 will include the incorporation of the plans submitted by EDS into
                 a    single    recovery plan.

                 In the future, the deliverables review process will ensure that
                 no payment is made for deliverables that have not received final
                 departmental         approval.

             The department ensure periodic and timely system capacity testing

             might have been earlier identified and corrections made if the

             deliverables,         thereby delaying the tests,              until they were
             useless, since the system had already experienced performance
             problems and system unavailability.  Management Systems staff
              then focused upon the identification and correction of production
              problems,        rather than modeling capacity needs.

              system development            RFPs.

           FINDING    #7


(66-68)    Significant increases in the department's Food Stamp Program
           quality control payment error rate occurred during the period of
           FLORIDA System conversion, thereby increasing the department's
           exposure to sanctions by the federal government.

           RECOMMENDATION #7


           The department implement corrective actions that will reduce its
           quality control payment error rates to acceptable levels.

           MANAGEMENT’S RESPONSE #7

           We concur. The newly formed FLORIDA Steering Committee, composed
           of technicians and users of the system, are embarking on a needs
           assessment of the entire entitlement program. This assessment
           will address the issues, shown below, which are contributing
           factors to entitlement error rates.  The result of the assessment
           will be short term and intermediate term recommendations to
           adequately address the needs required to run the entitlement
           programs effectively.

           e   Equitable Pay

           e   Excessive Turnover

           e   Training

           e   Staffing     for Case   Loads

           e   Tools to Do the Job

           e   Computer Capacity to Handle Case Loads

           Based on the recommendations, 1993/94 budget amendments and a
           1994/95 legislative budget request will be developed to address
           the   needs.

             Additionally, in September 1992, a corrective action plan was
             developed that will reduce the quality control error rates to an
             acceptable level.  The Blueprint for Improved Client Service to
             Public Assistance Clients Through the Reduction of Agency-Caused
             Errors is a comprehensive document which seeks reduction of
             errors in all public assistance programs by addressing two major
             areas of concern; the accuracy of the benefit amount and the
             timeliness of issuing benefits.  There are six main goals for
             improving the accuracy of issuing benefits and five goals for
             improving the timeliness of issuing benefits.

             Several goals are near completion and additional steps have been
             added as we move forward in the quest for continuous quality
(66-68)      improvement.  The results of some of the actions taken should
             begin to have a positive impact on the error rate during the
             second half of          federal    fiscal    year, March to     September   1993.

             Factors beyond the certification extension issue also contributed
             to the increased QC error rate.   One of the most significant
             factors is the continuing caseload increase in Florida which
             began in 1989 prior to any certification extensions in 1991 and
             1992.     Unfortunately,          the caseloads were not rapidly growing when
             the original RFP was prepared.  The caseload growth trends have
             been significant nationally; Florida is recognized among those
             states impacted tremendously by the declining economy.  During
             this time of spiraling caseload growth of 162.5% from 1987-1992,
             staffing increases in the public assistance programs only grew
              3.7%.

 Audit                                     AUDIT OF THE FLORIDA SYSTEM
Report
Par.

_ No.           MANAGEMENT’S RESPONSE #10

                We concur.     Management Systems staff recognize the need to
                dramatically improve Production Problem Report (PPR) resolution
                times and have begun to design an alternative systematic process
                to re-test critical portions of the system incorporating quality
                 control principles.      This re-test process will separate
                 efficiency programming from corrective programming, allowing
                 focus on certain modules or subsystems for complete redesign,
                 rather than continual correction of existing problems.  It will
                 place testing in the hands of expert program office policy
                 personnel rather than subjecting the user to continually writing
                 PPR's which interfere with their productivity.  It will also
                 ensure a comprehensive approach to testing rather than the
                 haphazard approach which results from receipt of PPR's on a flow
                 basis, and it will build a strong foundation for systematic
                 testing of future modifications.  We plan to use a formally
(83-87)          developed set of comprehensive test case scenarios designed to
                 test all outcomes of specific areas of functionality.

                 Additionally, Grant Thornton, an independent consulting certified
                 public accounting firm, will provide technical assistance to
                 Management Systems staff in developing the test scenarios.

                 We are developing an initiative to move system maintenance and
                 development from the mainframe to personal computers (PC's) and
                 to use PC programming productivity tools.   This will increase
                 programmer/analyst productivity and reduce the load on the
                 systen.


                 Additionally, we are re-evaluating all task priorities and staff
                 assignments so that we may maximize human resources.              The HRS
                 Reorganization Work group has allocated positions to monitor and
  LL             facilitate PPR review, prioritization and communication.


                 FINDING #11


(89-92)          The department had not established adequate computer programmed
                 controls within its FLORIDA System to ensure the reasonableness
                 and completeness of data received through data exchanges.

                 RECOMMENDATION #11

                 The department establish and maintain appropriate controls to
                 provide reasonable assurance of the reliability and accuracy of
                 information received through data exchanges.  These controls
                 should include, at a minimum, a check of input record totals
                 against independently provided record counts.
